| M4.2 | Lock, then check as a SEPARATE statement, then write, then commit. The check that decides starts after the lock is held | Putting the check in the locking query's WHERE clause, or trusting the prefilter that ran before the lock | Under READ COMMITTED every statement takes a fresh snapshot, so only a statement that starts after the lock sees what the previous lock holder committed. A check already running when the lock was granted can be stale. Proven by `test_a_stale_prefilter_cannot_create_a_second_offer` (event-gated, no sleeps): removing the check after the lock fails it. The prefilter stays, as a cheap way to avoid useless locks |
| M4.2 | Matching SKIPS a candidate whose lock is held and tries the next one (`SKIP LOCKED`); it never waits | Waiting for the lock (`FOR UPDATE` plain) | Someone else is deciding about that driver right now. Waiting would line up every rider in the city behind the nearest driver and turn matching into a queue; skipping keeps every request independent. The fan-out test and the stress runs show no match is lost (60 of 60 offers) because every locked driver ends with an offer |
| M4.2 | Rider and accept requests WAIT for the lock up to `LOCK_WAIT_MS` (3 s) and then answer 503 `Busy, please retry` | 409 on contention; waiting forever | These concern ONE specific person, so waiting is short and rare (a rider tapping twice, a driver double-clicking), and the second request then sees the first one's result and answers with the normal 409. | M4.2 | The rider lock is taken AFTER `estimate_ride`, so it is held only for the insert and the commit, and the unlocked first check stays as the cheap early exit | Locking at the top of `create_ride` | A lock must never be held across an OSRM call (hundreds of milliseconds, and a hung OSRM would hold the rider's row). What Redis was missing: F2 showed that a lock that expires inside its critical section breaks I1 in 7 of 10 rounds (no fencing token), and F1 that a crashed holder blocks a free driver for the full 10 s TTL. What Postgres costs: it can hold a connection while it waits, and a holder stuck mid-query keeps the lock (F1) |

| M4.2 | Blocking locks only in the order ride, offer, driver (accept) or user alone (create_ride); every lock taken while another is held in matching is non-blocking | A global lock order for all rows | Without a blocking edge from matching to anything, no cycle can form. | M5.1 | Rides settled before this milestone are marked `{"kind": "legacy"}` by the migration | Leaving them NULL; inventing fares | Invariants I8 and I9 and later receipts can tell "never settled" from "settlement missing" |
| M5.1 | The breakdown stores amounts, not rates | Storing the rule | A later rule change must not change old rides. A per-ride rule snapshot is not built (M5.2's surge needs its own locked multiplier) |
| M5.1 | Haversine is written inline in `record_trip_point` | A `utils/geo.py` function | It has one caller; CLAUDE.md allows a utility only when two places need it |
| M5.2 | Zones are geohash cells of the PICKUP at precision 5, with an in-house `geohash_encode` in `utils/geo.py` (about 4.9 x 4.8 km, 72 zones for the city box) | A geohash or H3 library; precision 6 (about 1.2 x 0.6 km); a PostGIS grid | The cell is close to the 3 km matching radius, a few dozen zones per city keep the snapshot small, no dependency is needed (the algorithm is 25 lines, checked against `pygeohash` with 0 mismatches in 2000 points), and a string zone is easy to index and to log. H3 would add equal-area hexagons (no shrinking width with latitude, uniform neighbors) and a neighbor lookup that removes the border effect; not needed for one city |
| M5.2 | Demand = unmet requests (REQUESTED and NO_DRIVER_FOUND) of DISTINCT riders created in the last 180 s | Counting every request; counting all statuses; a longer window | A rider who was served already used up a driver, a rider who cancelled is not waiting, one account spamming counts once; 180 s is long enough for a person to try again and short enough to forget a quiet spell |
| M5.2 | Supply = available drivers by matching's own definition (`get_available_ids`) whose current position is in the zone | Counting every online driver; a looser definition | The multiplier should answer "could a request here be served", which is exactly what matching decides; the test mutation (every online driver) proves it matters |
| M5.2 | Pressure is an integer percent (`demand * 100 // max(supply, 1)`) read through a step table, with `MIN_DEMAND_FOR_SURGE = 3` | A continuous formula (demand / supply); floats | Steps make quotes stable and testable by hand (the ladder run matches the table exactly); the minimum stops one unmatched rider from triggering surge |
| M5.2 | The cap is read from the pricing rule at lookup time (`max(100, int(surge_cap * 100 + 0.5))`), so the snapshot stores the uncapped values | Storing the capped value; a constant cap | A changed rule takes effect at once and M6.2 can edit the cap without touching the cache; the cap test proves the snapshot stays at 200 |
| M5.2 | One city-wide snapshot shared through Redis (`surge:snapshot`, 15 s TTL), no lock against duplicate recomputes | A computation per request; a per-zone cache; a lock or a background task | The cost no longer depends on the request rate (6 to 19 ms with 40 drivers online), everyone in a zone sees the same multiplier, 15 s of staleness is accepted, and a duplicate recompute at expiry is harmless (measured: 5 recomputes for 4 windows across two processes) |
| M5.2 | Surge multiplies the WHOLE normal fare after the minimum fare is applied: `(normal_fare * surge_percent + 50) // 100` | Surging only the distance and time parts; rounding down | Simple to explain and to check by hand; the rider sees one multiplier on one number; half up is the rule of the rest of the fare code |
| M5.2 | The multiplier is locked on the ride at request time (`rides.surge_percent`, `pickup_zone`) and settlement reuses it; the 150 percent cap works on the surged estimate | Re-reading the snapshot at completion; locking the whole rule | The rider agreed to that price; the current zone state at completion is irrelevant to them. Mutation (a) proves the test notices |
| M5.2 | `accepted_surge_percent` on `POST /rides`: 409 only when the price went UP, a lower price is charged as it is, absent means "accept the current one" | Always charging the accepted value; 409 on any change; a mandatory field | A rider is never charged more than they saw, never loses a cheaper price, and scripts, the simulators and the stress tool keep working without the field |
| M5.2 | The multiplier is read BEFORE the ride exists (so a rider's own request is not part of their own price) and before any lock | Reading it after the insert; reading it under the user lock | The first request into an empty zone must not raise its own price, and a lock must never be held across extra Redis and Postgres reads |
| M5.2 | A quote never fails because of Redis: a `RedisError` gives one warning and multiplier 100 | Failing the estimate with 503 | The estimate is informational; creating the ride still needs Redis for matching and answers 503 as before. Postgres errors are not caught |
| M5.2 | Cancellation fees are never surged | Surging the fee | The fee is a flat penalty for a wasted trip, not a price for a ride that happened; mutation (i) in three variants is caught |
| M5.2 | A check constraint keeps `surge_percent` between 100 and 200 | No constraint; a range in code only | A cheap database-level guard behind the 100 to 200 limits of the snapshot and of the request field; it is why there is no invariant for the range |
| M5.2 | An autouse fixture sets `MIN_DEMAND_FOR_SURGE` to a huge number for the whole suite; `test_surge.py` restores 3 and turns the cache off | Updating every old test for a surge-aware fare; patching in every test | The 328 older tests keep testing what they tested; surge tests state their own world explicitly |
| M5.2 | `GET /admin/surge` shows the snapshot (sorted, with its age) and `refresh=true` recomputes | No endpoint; a page | Observability for the demos, the stress runs and M6.2, without a new UI. (Resolved in M6.2: the Live tab shows its top 10 zones.) |
| M5.2 | Counts only, no ML: demand and supply, a step table, a cap | A learned or smoothed multiplier | The project has no ML layer; the learning goal is the data flow (snapshot, lock-in, idempotent settlement), not the forecast |
| M5.3 | The wallet is an append-only ledger (`wallet_entries`, each row with `balance_after`) plus `wallets.balance` updated under the wallet row lock; I13 and I14 check that the two agree | Computing the balance with `SUM` on every read; a balance column with no ledger | A stored balance is a cheap read and a row to lock (that is what serializes concurrent changes); the ledger is the audit trail. Two copies of one fact can disagree, so two invariants guard it, and no code except `post_entry` writes either |
| M5.3 | Wallet rows are created lazily (`INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, then a column-only `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`); reading never creates one | A wallet created at registration | No change to registration, no backfill, drivers and admins never get one; two first writers cannot create two rows |
| M5.3 | The reservation is derived from the rider's one active wallet ride (the fare cap), never stored | Stored holds and releases | The unique active-ride index means at most one hold exists; nothing to release, nothing to leak. A wallet ride needs `balance >= cap` at request and nothing else lowers the balance during the ride, so the charge can always be covered |
| M5.3 | The cap (150 percent of the estimate, `pricing.fare_cap`) is both the largest possible charge and the amount the wallet must hold | A separate buffer or a fixed amount | The rider can never be charged more than was checked; one function defines it for settlement, estimate, reservation and request check |
| M5.3 | Top-ups use Stripe Checkout, the hosted page | Stripe.js, Elements, Payment Intents | No card data touches our server or our pages; the whole card UI is Stripe's; fewer moving parts to learn first |
| M5.3 | No `stripe` package: `httpx` and `hmac` | The official SDK | The SDK would add typed objects, retries, a pinned API version and a signature helper; the project calls two endpoints and checks one header, which is about 60 lines and teaches the protocol (and keeps "no new dependencies"). The cost: the API version is not pinned |
| M5.3 | Two idempotency layers on top-up creation: our unique (user, key) and a Stripe key `topup-<our id>` derived from our row, with the row COMMITTED before the Stripe call | A random Stripe key per attempt; holding a transaction open across the call | A retry after a crash between "row stored" and "session stored" gets the SAME session, two simultaneous requests cannot make two sessions, and no lock is held across a network call |
| M5.3 | The Checkout `expires_at` is the row's `created_at` plus an hour, not now | `now + 1 hour` | Found while writing the fake: Stripe compares the parameters of a retry with the same key, so a retry must send the same form. The cost: a retry more than 30 minutes after the row was created is refused by Stripe (502), and the rider starts a new top-up |
| M5.3 | Webhook dedup is the first statement of processing and shares the transaction with the credit | Recording the event after the commit; dedup by session only | A crash in the middle rolls the event id back too, so Stripe's retry is processed instead of thrown away; the dedup row and the credit cannot disagree |
| M5.3 | One `credit_topup` for the webhook and for `sync`: lock the top-up row, re-check its status as a separate statement, credit only if not SUCCEEDED | Two code paths with their own checks | A different event id for the same session, or sync racing the webhook, cannot credit twice. The unique index is only the safety net (shown by switching the guards off) |
| M5.3 | We trust our own row (session id, amount, currency) and never the event's metadata or `amount_total` alone | Crediting what the event says | The event proves a payment happened; our row says what it was for. Mutation (i) catches the other way |
| M5.3 | Events that do not match answer 200 `ignored` (and are recorded); a bad signature is 400; only a real failure is a 500 | 4xx for unknown events | Stripe retries non-2xx answers for days; an event we deliberately ignore must not trigger that |
| M5.3 | A top-up that EXPIRED but turns out to be paid is credited | Refusing it | The money was taken; refusing would keep the rider's money without giving the credit |
| M5.3 | Only test keys (`sk_test_`, `rk_test_`) are ever used; anything else counts as not configured, with one ERROR at startup | Trusting whatever key is configured | A safety rule for a learning project: a live key pasted into `.env` by mistake charges nobody |
| M5.3 | Cash rides are assumed collected by the driver (a payment row with method cash, no ledger entry) | Modelling cash collection | There is no driver-side money model before M5.4; the row keeps I16 uniform for every settled ride |
| M5.3 | Completing and cancelling take no idempotency key | A key on `/complete` and `/cancel` | A repeat is already answered by the state machine (409) under the ride lock, and the payment's key is derived from the ride, so a second charge is impossible |
| M5.3 | The charge is the last write in `cancel` (after the pending-offer handling), not right after the settlement call | Charging directly after `settle_cancelled_ride` | `post_entry` must be the last write before the commit so the wallet lock stays a leaf; in practice a REQUESTED ride (the only one with a pending offer) has a fee of 0 |
| M5.3 | Admin adjustments (`POST /admin/wallets/{id}/adjust`) take the wallet lock before looking for the key, with a limit of Rs 10,000 | An IntegrityError handler; unlimited amounts | Requests with one key take turns and the second sees the first, so no unique violation can happen; the limit bounds a typo. It is the support and development way to fund a wallet |
| M5.3 | Payments for rides settled before this milestone are backfilled in the migration (cash, succeeded) | Leaving old rides without payments and excluding them from I16 | I16 holds for every ride, not just the new ones; the backfill count and sum were checked against the numbers measured before |
| M5.3 | `extra_hosts: host.docker.internal:host-gateway` in compose, and `fake_stripe.py` as a dev tool on the host | Stripe in a container; only real Stripe | The container can reach a fake on the host on Linux; the fake allows races, replays and crashes to be staged without an account. It does not model declines, refunds, API versions or expiry by the clock |
| M5.4 | Commission is an integer percent in the pricing rule (`commission_percent`, 0 to 100, default 20), snapshotted on every earning row at settlement | A float rate; reading the current rule when a view is asked for; a rate on the driver | Integer percents keep the money integer; a stored snapshot means a later rule edit never rewrites history and summaries are plain sums |
| M5.4 | `platform_fee` is half up and the driver gets the remainder, backed by the CHECK `split_adds_up` | Rounding both parts | Rounding never creates or loses a paisa; mutation (a) shows the rounding is tested |
| M5.4 | A separate `ride_earnings` table with unique links to the ride and to the payment | Columns on `payments`; computing the split on read | The rider side is `payments`, the split is its own fact (and a different reader); the unique constraints make a duplicate impossible and I19 can join the two |
| M5.4 | The earning row is written by `charge_ride`, right after the payment and before the wallet entry, in the same transaction | A later job; a separate request | A payment without its split cannot exist even for an instant, so I19 and I20 hold at every snapshot; `post_entry` stays the last write so the wallet row remains a leaf lock |
| M5.4 | A cancellation fee is split with the same percent | No commission on fees; a separate percent | The driver is paid for the wasted trip, and one rule is easier to state; logged as an open issue because it is a simple choice |
| M5.4 | Commission is taken on the whole payment, surge included | Commission on the normal fare only | The rider pays one amount; the platform's share of every payment is one percent |
| M5.4 | Settlement is derived from the rows: cash means the driver owes the commission, wallet means the platform owes the earning | A balance table; one total | There are no payouts yet, so a stored balance would only be a second copy of the rows; two directions are different money |
| M5.4 | Windows are explicit timestamps (`since` inclusive, `until` exclusive, with a timezone) built from the browser's local midnight | A server timezone setting, `tzdata`, `?day=` | The server needs no timezone data and no setting; a naive time is a 422 instead of a silent guess |
| M5.4 | Every summary is ONE grouped query, folded by one helper used by three callers | Several queries (counts, then sums) | The totals of one answer always agree with each other, even while rides settle |
| M5.4 | Receipts are rider-only and derived from the stored breakdown, payment and ledger entry, with a deterministic number (`RCPT-` and the padded ride id) and a fallback for breakdowns from before M5.2 | Recomputing from the rule; a receipts table; random numbers | A later rule or surge change cannot change a receipt (mutation (g)); no extra table to keep in step; the number needs no storage |
| M5.4 | No commission or earning data in any rider-facing response; the driver's entries show the addresses of their own rides and nothing about the rider | One shared ride view | The rider pays one amount and does not need the platform's share; the driver sees where they drove, not who |
| M5.4 | The driver page loads earnings at the start, on the buttons and once when a ride finishes, never on the 3 second poll | Refreshing with every poll | Earnings change only when a ride is settled; the browser run proved no request on polls |
| M5.4 | The backfill of earlier payments is a guarded statement: the migration raises when a payment is left without a row | A silent backfill | The migration fails loudly (and rolls back) instead of leaving gaps that I19 would report later |
| M5.4 | The chaos scenario compares the API with its own SQL (admin revenue, every stress driver, 20 receipts), once more after 2 s | Trusting the unit tests | The views are read under concurrent settlement; a view that disagrees with the rows is a defect the invariants cannot see |
| M5.4 | The admin endpoints are API-only | An admin page now | The dashboard is M6.2. (Resolved in M6.2: the Overview and Drivers tabs show them.) |
| M6.1 | A separate `rating_summaries` table (`rating_count`, `rating_total`) changed by one atomic upsert in the rating's transaction, guarded by I21 | `AVG(score)` on every read; a column on `users` | An average over a growing table is slower with every rating and is needed on every ride; the upsert never reads, so two ratings of one user cannot lose an update, and I21 compares the table with the ratings so a bug cannot hide |
| M6.1 | Integer count and total, and an integer average in hundredths rounded half up (`utils/ratings.average`) | Floats; Python's `round()` | `round(4.625, 2)` is 4.62 (halves go to even), the money code already rounds half up, and integers can be compared exactly in SQL and in the chaos cross-check |
| M6.1 | One rating per rater per ride, enforced by the existing unique constraint and `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING`; ratings are final | An `IntegrityError` handler; editing within a window | No row back is the answer "already rated", with no exception to catch; final ratings need no edit history or moderation |
| M6.1 | Only COMPLETED rides can be rated, for 7 days after `completed_at` (a constant read at call time) | Cancelled rides with a fee; no limit | A trip that never happened has nothing to rate; a window stops very late revenge or cleanup ratings; reading the constant at call time lets a test move it |
| M6.1 | No ride lock; the summary row is a leaf lock taken as the last write | `FOR UPDATE` on the ride | A COMPLETED ride and its `completed_at` never change, so there is nothing to protect; the summary row is the only contention and nothing else is locked while it is held |
| M6.1 | Individual ratings and comments are hidden from the person who was rated; the rater sees only their own | Showing received ratings | A rider or driver who can see who rated them badly can retaliate; averages are enough feedback |
| M6.1 | Someone else's average is hidden below 3 ratings (the count is shown) | Showing every average; Bayesian smoothing | One or two ratings are too easy to trace to a person; smoothing needs a prior and is a product decision |
| M6.1 | `average` and the public minimum live in `utils/ratings.py` | A function in `services/ratings.py` | `services/ratings` imports `services/rides`, so `services/rides` (the driver details) cannot import it back; it is pure and used in two places |
| M6.2 | The existing `GET /admin/drivers` was extended in place (paging, search, `AdminDriverResponse` with more fields) | A second endpoint | One list of drivers, and its only caller was the admin page; no test asserted the key set, so no test changed because of it (the 14 that changed were caused by the new database checks, see "Admin dashboard results") |
| M6.2 | Pricing edits use an optimistic `version` column and an append-only audit table; a stale version is 409 | Locking the row across a form session; a pessimistic "edit lock" | A form is open for minutes and an admin may leave it open: a lock held that long would block settlements, and the version check loses nothing |
| M6.2 | A replay of an applied edit fails with 409, so no `Idempotency-Key` | An idempotency key like top-ups | The version already makes the second copy stale; a key would add a table and a second way to say the same thing |
| M6.2 | A patch that changes nothing writes nothing (200, `changes: []`, no version bump, no audit row) | Bumping the version anyway | The history stays a list of real changes, and a double click cannot add rows |
| M6.2 | The bounds are API constants (`RULE_LIMITS`) returned to the page; only the safety floors (`>= 0`, `min_fare >= 100`) and the `surge_cap` range (1.0 to 2.0, the same as `rides.surge_percent`) are database checks | Every bound as a check; bounds hard-coded in the page | The floors keep every trip chargeable even for a direct SQL edit, the cap range must match another check, and the upper bounds can be raised without a migration; the page never needs a second copy of a range |
| M6.2 | The surge steps, the minimum demand, the 150 percent fare cap, the matching radius and the offer timeout stay constants | Moving them into the rule | They are logic and tuning of the algorithms, and editing them while rides run needs more design than an editor; the cap and the rates are the numbers an operator changes |
| M6.2 | An edit applies to rides already in progress at completion (the whole current rule, so base fare and minimum fare too), capped at 150 percent of the ride's own estimate; the page says so and shows how many rides are active | Freezing the rule on every ride at request time | Settlement already read the rule at completion and the cap already protects the rider; freezing would need a new column and a migration for rules. The first draft of the page text said "per-km and per-minute" only: reading the code showed that base fare and minimum fare are re-read too, so the text says so |
| M6.2 | The live snapshot is plain reads (Redis, then unlocked Postgres statements), with exact totals, row caps and no claim of atomicity; a driver's state is computed as active ride, else offline, else offered, else free | A lock or a transaction around Redis and Postgres; a state from presence first | There is no way to lock Redis and Postgres together, and a read-only view must not block ride flows. A driver whose presence key expired during a ride is still on a ride, so the ride comes first; a pending offer only matters for a driver who is online and has no ride |
| M6.2 | Redis down is a 503 for the map and the drivers list (they need presence) but only a null `online_drivers` in the stats | A 503 everywhere; zeros | A map without positions is meaningless, but the stats are mostly Postgres and must stay available |
| M6.2 | Stats have two cohorts: rides by request time, money by settlement time; rates use the finished rides as the denominator; averages and rates are integer half-up | One cohort; floats; all rides as the denominator | A payment settled today can belong to a ride requested yesterday, and the M5.4 views were already by settlement time; an active ride has no outcome yet; floats round halves to even (1 of 16 must show 6.3) |
| M6.2 | Buckets use a fixed UTC offset sent by the browser and plain arithmetic; the series is zero-filled in the service | A timezone database in the backend; gaps in the series | No new dependency and no zone data to keep; the browser knows its own offset (wrong for one side of a daylight saving change, listed in the open issues); a chart needs every bucket, and doing it in the service keeps the SQL a plain group by |
| M6.2 | The charts are hand-drawn inline SVG (`createElementNS`, one `drawBars`), the map uses `preferCanvas` and no marker clustering | A chart library; a clustering plugin | No new dependency (the project has none besides Leaflet); a canvas renderer is enough for a thousand dots |
| M6.2 | A clicked marker shows its details in a side panel (`textContent`); tooltips are a fixed word and an integer id | Leaflet popups with the details | Leaflet renders tooltip and popup content as HTML, so no API string may reach it |
| M6.2 | Polling cadence per tab: Live and Drivers every 3 s, Overview every 9 s, an open unfinished ride every 3 s, the rides list and the pricing form never; polling continues in a background tab | One cadence for all; polling the pricing form | The map must feel live, stats are heavier and change slowly, a list the admin is reading must not move under them, and a poll must never overwrite an edit in a form |
| M6.2 | The URL hash is read once, with one strict regex, and only digits and the five tab names are ever used from it | Reading `hashchange`; building anything from the hash | Nothing from the URL can reach the page except an id and a tab name. The cost: editing the hash in the address bar does nothing until the page is reloaded |
| M6.2 | `frontend/admin/admin.js` stays ONE long file, grouped by tab | Splitting it into modules | An approved exception for this milestone: the page has one `state` and one `render()`, and splitting them would add an interface to keep in sync |
| M6.2 | The chaos run edits the pricing rule about every 10 s (30 percent of the edits twice at once), checks the admin views for structure, cross-checks stats, rides, drivers and details against SQL after the settle period, and restores the original rule with one `PATCH` | Only unit tests; leaving the rule changed | The editor and the views are read and written under real concurrency; the stress tool must leave the dev rule as it found it |
| M6.2 | The drivers list uses a correlated count of completed trips per returned row | A grouped subquery over all rides | The page asks for 50 rows: the count runs through `ix_rides_driver_id` for 50 drivers instead of scanning every ride |
| M6.2 | `lock_rule` returns only the rule id, and the version is read and compared in separate statements after the lock | Returning `(id, version)` from the locking query | The Phase 4 rule: the check must be a statement that starts after the lock |
| M6.1 | The driver details endpoint carries the public rating | A new rider-side endpoint | The rider page already asks for those details once per ride, so no new request or endpoint is needed |
| M6.1 | Ratings are fetched over REST once per finished ride, with no event | A `rating_requested` event; fetching on every poll | An event would need a WebSocket protocol change and a missed one is harmless; the key set before the call keeps a failure from repeating on every 3 second poll |
| M6.1 | Comments are private to their author and to admins, at most 300 characters (a CHECK, a schema limit and `maxlength`) | Public comments | A free text field is the part most likely to be abusive or to identify someone |
| M6.1 | The admin ratings list is API-only | A page now | The dashboard is M6.2; the list pages with `before_id` like the other lists. (Resolved in M6.2: the driver detail and the ride detail show ratings and comments as text.) |
| M6.1 | The chaos scenario compares `GET /ratings/me` and the driver details with its own SQL, once more after 2 s | Trusting the unit tests | The views are read under concurrent ratings; a view that disagrees with the rows is a defect the invariants cannot see |
| M6.1 | The `ratings` scenario puts every rating on one driver | Spreading the ratings over many drivers | It concentrates contention on one summary row, the worst case of the upsert |

| M6.3 | Two role-specific endpoints, `GET /rides/history` and `GET /drivers/me/history` | One `/history` route that switches its row shape on the caller's role | The two shapes share almost nothing (a rider sees a driver name, a driver sees an earning split and nothing about the rider). A route that switches would make the privacy rule a runtime branch instead of two schemas that are the allow-list |
| M6.3 | History lists finished rides only, ordered and paged by ride id (request order), with `since` and `until` on the request time | Listing active rides too; ordering by completion time | The ride view already shows the active ride. Ride ids are strictly increasing, so `before_id` is a stable cursor with no ties; completion time would need an index and a tie-breaker. The cost (a long trip finishes after a short one that was requested later) is an open issue |
| M6.3 | One query per page: an EXISTS for the payment, outer joins for the caller's rating, the driver's name and the earning, a scalar subquery for `ended_at`; a test counts the SQL statements of a page of 5 and of 50 and requires the same number (2 for a rider, 5 for a driver) | A query per row; loading the related rows afterwards with `IN` | Rows per page are bounded, but a query per row would be 20 to 50 round trips per request. The statement-count test is what stops an N+1 from coming back |
| M6.3 | `ended_at` is the time of the ride's LAST event, `distance_m` and `duration_s` are `COALESCE(actual_*, estimate)` | `completed_at` for `ended_at` (null for cancelled rides); the estimate only | A cancelled ride has no `completed_at`, and its last event is the terminal transition for every finished status. The tracked values are what was billed. The cost is that `ended_at` and `completed_at` are redundant for COMPLETED rides |
| M6.3 | A rider sees the driver's NAME and nothing else about the driver; a driver sees nothing about the rider | Showing the vehicle or the plate on the row; the rider's first name to the driver | The receipt and the driver details already show the vehicle where it belongs. Nothing about the rider means the history can never leak contact data or a rating by construction |
| M6.3 | `can_rate` repeats the rating rule, and a test pins the two together | Asking the rating endpoint for every row | That would be one request per row. The test rides the boundary (6 days 23 hours, exactly 7 days, 7 days 1 hour, with the window also patched to 2 days) for the rider and the driver and requires `can_rate` to equal "the rating endpoint answers 201" |
| M6.3 | No detail endpoint: a past trip is opened through the existing finished-ride view and its endpoints (`/rides/{id}`, `/events`, `/driver`, `/receipt`, `/rating`) | A history-specific detail endpoint and view | The receipt, the rating form, the route, the earnings line and the timeline already exist and are tested; a second copy would drift. Open only has to set the remembered ride and clear the per-ride data, and `refresh()` does the rest |
| M6.3 | Opening a past trip is refused while the person has a ride (rider) or a ride or a pending offer (driver); the offer panel stays visible in every view | Replacing the active ride in the view; hiding the offer panel outside the Drive view | The finished-ride view shows one remembered ride and an active ride takes its place. A driver must still be able to answer an offer from My trips, or the countdown would run out unseen |
| M6.3 | Saved places are text snapshots with no geocoding, no link to rides, and a bounds check before the lock | Calling Nominatim to refresh an address; a foreign key from rides | The public Nominatim server allows one request per second, and deleting a place must never touch a trip. The bounds check is cheap and needs no lock |
| M6.3 | Saved places create and rename lock the owner's `users` row first (plain blocking, a leaf lock), check the count and the label as separate statements, then write; the cap is enforced by the lock | A counter column or trigger; a CHECK (the database cannot count rows); a Redis lock | Phase 4 rule: lock, check in a new statement, write, commit. A counter would be a second thing to keep equal to the rows. Delete takes no lock because removing a row can never break the cap or the labels. The same Postgres row is the one `users.lock` takes for ride creation, so the two wait for each other, which is short and harmless |
| M6.3 | Labels are unique per rider ignoring case through the expression index `uq_saved_places_user_id_lower_label` on `(user_id, lower(label))`, and the check uses `lower()` too; the bounds check precedes the lock | A case-sensitive label; a `citext` column (a new extension) | "Home" and "home" are the same place to a person. The index is the safety net and its violation is not caught: it would mean the lock is missing. Alembic autogenerate compares the expression index correctly (an empty revision afterwards) |
| M6.3 | The chaos run cross-checks `GET /saved-places` and the history lists of every stress rider and driver against its own SQL (walked page by page, retried once after 2 s), and the cleanup deletes the stress riders' saved places through the API | Trusting the unit tests; leaving the places in place | The lists are read under concurrent writes, and a list that disagrees with the rows is a defect the invariants cannot see. Without the cleanup the chaos riders would sit at the limit of 10 for ever |
| M6.3 | No polling or events for the trip lists and the saved places; they load when the view opens, on a filter change, on Refresh and on Load more | A `ride_finished` event; polling the list | REST is the source of truth and a list is not time critical. A poll would repeat a paged query every 3 seconds per open page |
| M6.3 | `choosePoint(kind, lat, lng, address)` is extracted from the per-result click handler and shared by the search results and the saved places; the map click keeps using `setPoint` alone | Copying the handler; making the map click zoom too | A saved place must behave exactly like a search result (marker, `setView(..., 16)`, address in the box, estimate). A map click is where the person pointed, so the map should not jump |
| M6.3 | "Back to my trips" and "Request a new ride" share one click handler (a loop over the two buttons) | A new helper function | They forget the finished ride in the same way, and CLAUDE.md allows no helper that is not needed in two places by name; a loop over two buttons needs none. The same for the driver's "Wait for a new ride" |
| M7.1 | JSON logs with the stdlib `logging` module and one custom `Formatter`, configured once in `setup_logging()` | structlog, python-json-logger, loguru, a text format | The project has no logging dependency and the whole behavior (whitelist, exception policy, context) is about 60 lines the owner can read. A text format cannot be filtered with `jq`. |
| M7.1 | A field whitelist in the formatter (23 keys); unknown `extra` keys are dropped silently; the list of things that are never logged is in CLAUDE.md | Trusting each call site; redacting by name | Redaction by name misses the next field; an allow-list fails closed. Adding a field means changing the list and its test, which is where a reviewer looks. |
| M7.1 | Exception messages are never logged: `exc_type`, `stack` (frames only), and for a database error `sqlstate` and `constraint` | `%r` of the exception, `exc_info` with the formatted message | SQLAlchemy messages carry the SQL parameters (an email in a unique violation) and httpx messages carry URLs (search text, coordinates). The cost is real: some failures are harder to debug (the type, the frames and the constraint name remain). The older call sites that logged `%r` or `error.orig` were changed to the type name or the SQLSTATE. |
| M7.1 | A pure ASGI middleware (a closure, not `BaseHTTPMiddleware`) and ONE mutable dict in a `ContextVar`; code changes the dict and never calls `set()` again | `BaseHTTPMiddleware`; setting individual context variables | `BaseHTTPMiddleware` runs the app in another task, so the context does not come back. A `set()` inside a threadpool dependency or the SQLAlchemy greenlet changes a COPY of the context and the access line would not see it (mutation (n) proves it); a change to the same dict is visible everywhere. The auth dependency gets one line. |
| M7.1 | The unhandled-exception 500 is sent by our middleware, which puts the exception type and stack on the access line | Letting Starlette's `ServerErrorMiddleware` send it; a separate log line | That middleware is outside ours: its 500 would have no `X-Request-ID`, and uvicorn would log the exception again (two ERROR lines). Ours gives one ERROR line with the request id and the same generic body. If the response already started we re-raise and uvicorn logs it. |
| M7.2 | A separate `postgres-load`, `redis-load` and `backend-load` (compose override file, `extends: backend`) | Using the dev stack with a throwaway database; a second compose project | A load run writes thousands of rows and flushes Redis: the dev data must never be at risk, and `extends` keeps the two backends from drifting. Needed `!override` for `ports` and `depends_on`, otherwise the extended service would publish 8000/8001 and start the dev db. |
| M7.2 | The load database is reset from a template database (`DROP`/`CREATE DATABASE ... TEMPLATE`, 3 s) and the backend restarts per run | Truncating tables; reusing the state | Every run starts with the same rows, empty rides and metric counters at 0, so the metrics can be compared with SQL exactly. |
| M7.2 | The request mix is the list found in the page scripts, with polling every 3 s as the dominant load; users are `FastHttpUser` with their own fixed-cadence loop | Guessed endpoint weights; Locust wait times | A wait after the requests drifts with latency and lowers the load exactly when the server is slow; a cadence like the page's `setInterval` does not. |
| M7.2 | A fixed 80-driver fleet (the unchanged simulator) and a NO_DRIVER_FOUND outcome mix | Scaling drivers with riders; calling no drivers a failure | Ride outcomes are reported, not errors. The fleet sends about 1 request per second per driver, fewer than the real driver page (5 per tick). |
| M7.2 | Geocoding, Stripe and WebSockets are not load tested; `backend-load` has no Stripe key and a dead Nominatim address | Calling the public services | Light-use servers and test money. The simulator opens no WebSocket, so there is no WebSocket traffic at all. |
| M7.2 | Step load with 30 s warm-up, 90 s hold, spawn rate 5 per second and a collapse stop (5 percent errors or p95 over 5 s, twice in a row) | Ramps; spawn rate 10 | Every login hashes a password for about 110 ms on the event loop, so 10 per second would block it completely. |
| M7.2 | SLOs on the p95 (and p99 for reads), never on the mean; the Locust CPU above 70 percent of a core makes a step INVALID | Mean latency; trusting every step | A mean hides the slow requests users feel; a saturated generator measures itself. |
| M7.2 | `event_loop_lag_seconds` (a 0.1 s sleep, the overshoot) | CPU % only | For one async process the lag says whether requests wait behind the loop; a histogram, so the report can take p95. |
| M7.2 | The harness scrapes `/metrics` itself and reads the access lines of the log for server-side numbers | Requiring Prometheus; new instrumentation | Nothing else has to run, and the per-request `db_ms` and `duration_ms` already exist. |
| M7.2 | Three baseline runs, and (planned, not done) three after-fix runs plus a revert run | One run each | Without the spread between runs an improvement cannot be told from noise. The baseline spread was tight (core p95 at 150 users 1847-2156 ms). |
| M7.2 | The one fix and its rejected alternatives were NOT decided | | Stopped before the experiments. Candidates the brief allows: pool settings, hashing in a threadpool, one cache or query rewrite on the path the evidence names. |

| M7.1 | `X-Request-ID` is kept only when it matches `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{8,64}$` (`fullmatch`), otherwise a `uuid4().hex` | Any inbound value; no inbound value | The id lands in every log line and a response header: a newline or a quote in it would forge or break lines. A client that wants to correlate can still send a plain id. |
| M7.1 | Metric labels are route templates (`scope["route"].path`), with `unmatched` (no route, or the frontend mount's 404) and `static`, and unknown methods are `OTHER` | The raw path as a label; per-ride labels | Cardinality: 1000 unknown paths and 200 ride ids added one series (25 to 26). Ids are in the logs, never in the metrics. |
| M7.1 | Ride transitions are counted and logged by a session hook after the outermost commit (`after_insert` on `RideEvent` noting the item, `after_commit`, `after_soft_rollback` for a rolled back savepoint, `after_transaction_end` for cleanup). The events are ORM objects, so `repositories/rides.py` was not edited | Counting inside `change_ride_status`; counting before the commit; the one-line Core insert variant | A count inside the state machine would be wrong whenever the transaction later rolls back (the settlement can: a missing pricing rule is a 503), and it would put observability code in the code that must stay plain. Findings from the probe: `after_commit` also fires for the RELEASE of a savepoint, and `after_rollback` also fires for a savepoint rollback, so neither is a clean signal; `after_soft_rollback` carries the rolled-back transaction and `after_transaction_end` of the root cleans up even for a session that is closed or reused. |
| M7.1 | Database state (`rides_active`, `offers_pending`, the pool, `drivers_online`) is read by a background task every 5 s with the existing repository functions, in two separate try blocks | Queries at scrape time; new queries | A scrape must cost nothing and cannot be slowed by a slow database; two blocks so a Redis outage keeps the Postgres gauges and the other way round. A failure leaves the old values and counts `observability_errors_total`. Business gauges are therefore up to 5 s old. |
| M7.1 | Offer outcomes are not counted (no `offers_total{outcome}`) | A counter per outcome | Not asked for, and the logs already carry one line per offer. Offer outcomes are visible only through `offers_pending` and the log lines. |
| M7.1 | `GET /metrics` is protected by `METRICS_TOKEN` (404 when empty, 401 for anything wrong, `hmac.compare_digest`) | An open endpoint; an IP allow-list; a user JWT | Metrics list routes and counts; the compose stack publishes port 8000 on all interfaces. A user JWT must never be enough, and an empty token must not mean open. |
| M7.1 | The compose profile `observability` with pinned images (`prom/prometheus:v3.14.0`, `grafana/grafana:13.2.3`), ports on 127.0.0.1, named volumes, and the token passed through the container environment (written to a file by the start command, read through `credentials_file`) | `latest` tags; a token inside `prometheus.yml`; a bind-mounted secret file | Reproducible and invisible to a plain `docker compose up`. The file keeps the token out of the repository; the cost is that `docker inspect` shows it. `${GRAFANA_ADMIN_PASSWORD:-}` instead of `${VAR:?}`: compose interpolates the whole file, so `:?` would break every command without the profile; the grafana start command refuses an empty password instead. |
| M7.1 | The dashboard is one hand-maintained JSON file written once (provisioned, not editable in the UI) with fixed histogram buckets | Importing a community dashboard; click setup; dynamic buckets | A dashboard that is part of the repository is reviewed like code and comes up populated. Buckets are fixed in `metrics.py` so quantiles are comparable over time. |
| M7.1 | `--no-access-log` plus our own `http_request` line; uvicorn's WebSocket `[accepted]` line is dropped by a handler filter; httpx, httpcore and websockets are pinned to WARNING | Keeping uvicorn's access log; redacting it | uvicorn prints full paths with query strings (and the client address for WebSockets); httpx logs every URL at INFO; websockets logs frames at DEBUG, including the first one, which carries the token. Found while building: the first two would have leaked without the filters. |
| M7.1 | The chaos run compares the metrics with the database (transitions exactly, `POST /rides` 201 as a LOWER bound, series and error counts, `rides_active` of the first process) and skips the counter checks when `process_start_time_seconds` changed | An exact HTTP comparison; asking the user whether a restart happened | Other clients may add requests, so only a lower bound is true; the transition counters are exact because every event is counted once after its commit. A restart resets the counters (and a crash between the commit and the hook loses a count), so the check says it was skipped instead of failing. `observability_errors_total` is compared as the INCREASE during the run, so an earlier outage does not fail a clean run. |
| M7.1 | A close that ends without a status is counted as 1006 (uvicorn reports it as 1005) | Counting 1005 as `other` | The abrupt drop is the case the 1006 label exists for; uvicorn gives the same 1005 for it and for an empty close frame, so they cannot be told apart. |
| M7.3 | The demo script lives inside `README.md` ("Demo script: two windows, one trip"); the older README sections were kept unchanged under a "# Reference" heading, and only the old intro and the "Start" section were replaced by the new top | A `docs/DEMO.md` (a new folder); shortening the reference sections | No new folder was needed, the reference sections are cross-referenced by name ("Create an admin", "Make surge for a demo"), and the brief asked to keep them. |
| M7.3 | The architecture diagram shows Redis as GEO positions, presence keys, pub/sub and caches, and says the ride locks are Postgres row locks | Drawing "Redis locks" | M4.2 chose Postgres row locks; the only Redis `SET NX` left is the Nominatim rate-limit slot. |
| M7.3 | The load test is documented as it is: capacity 100 users, knee 150 users, the event loop as a hypothesis, the full pool as the open question, no fix | Calling the event loop the confirmed cause; stating a number of concurrent rides | The three experiments were not run, and no concurrent-rides figure was measured (the numbers are in users, 30 percent of them requesting rides). |

## Bugs hit

One entry per notable bug: milestone, symptom, root cause, fix.

- **M7.2:** findings of the harness validation, none a bug of the app's behavior under normal load: (1) **a Redis outage of 10 s makes unrelated reads 2 to 6 s slow:** the fleet's location pings wait about 3 s for Redis while holding a database connection (the request-scoped session), the 15-connection pool runs dry and rider reads, which never touch Redis, queue behind them (1 error but a p99 of 5.8 s; the server counted 29 5xx, all from pings); not fixed. (2) **The 400-user step first ended the run early:** two logins timed out (20 s, the loop busy) and the login helper quit the whole run; now a failed login is retried, and a user that is stopped does not cut a request in flight (`--stop-timeout 30`). (3) **A crashed task restarted at once** (124 requests per second instead of 38 during a Postgres outage): a failed `GET /rides/active` set the remembered ride to `None`; fixed, and tasks now wait one tick before a restart. (4) `backend.log.gz` was truncated because both the log follower and `gzip` were terminated; now only the follower. (5) The `POST /rides` count check failed by one in the first baseline: a request was in flight when Locust killed its users; the check now allows requests without an answer. (6) A manually fired Locust event has no `response` argument; the listener raised and Locust swallowed it, so the OUTCOME events were missing from the log for one run.
- **M7.1:** no bug in the behavior of the shipped app was found by the tests, the 17 mutation checks, the stress and chaos runs; findings and slips on the way: (1) **uvicorn and the libraries would have leaked personal data through the new root handler:** routing uvicorn's loggers through one handler made `uvicorn.error` print `"WebSocket /ws?..." [accepted]` (client address and the path with its query string), httpx print every request URL at INFO, and websockets print every frame at DEBUG (the auth frame holds the token). Found by reading the library sources and by the privacy test at DEBUG. Fix: a handler filter and WARNING for `httpx`, `httpcore`, `websockets`. (2) **`after_commit` fires for a savepoint release and `after_rollback` fires for a savepoint rollback** (probe script), so the first design (clear everything in `after_rollback`) would have dropped events of the outer transaction; fixed with `after_soft_rollback` (carries the rolled-back transaction) and `after_transaction_end` of the root. (3) **a test-support trap:** `uvicorn.Config(log_level="warning")` in the `live_server` fixture resets uvicorn's handlers and sets `uvicorn.error` to WARNING, which hid the INFO WebSocket lines from the log capture; the capture fixture now puts them back and must be requested after `open_socket`. (4) **my first audit fix crashed:** logging `error.orig.sqlstate` in `matching.py` raised `AttributeError` for the synthetic `IntegrityError` of an existing test (a log line turned a skipped candidate into a 500). It now uses `getattr`, and logs the constraint name too (another test asserts it). (5) **nine existing tests relied on the ASGI transport re-raising an unhandled exception** (see "Observability results"). (6) **uvicorn reports an abrupt WebSocket drop as code 1005, not 1006:** the first version counted it as `other`. (7) A stray `pkill -f` pattern matched my own shell command twice and killed it (no effect on the repository). (8) The dev Redis lost its ephemeral state when `docker compose --profile observability down` was run on purpose to check what it does (it removes the whole project).
- **M6.3:** three findings, none a bug that reached the data. (1) **A mutation hung the suite.** The first paging tests looped `while True` until a page came back empty; with `before_id` made inclusive (`<=`) a page repeats its last row for ever, so the mutation run hung instead of failing. Fix: the paging loops in `test_history.py` are bounded (`for _ in range(10)` with `pytest.fail` in the `else`). (2) **The upper length checks on `saved_places` can never fire.** `VARCHAR(30)` and `VARCHAR(200)` refuse a 31 or 201 character text with "value too long" before the CHECK is evaluated, so the tests for those two cases assert the column type error and only the empty text reaches `ck_saved_places_label_length` and `ck_saved_places_address_length`. (3) **A rename could answer 500 when a delete committed between its read and its update.** The delete takes no lock, so `UPDATE ... RETURNING` could find no row; `rename` now returns `None` and the service answers 404, found by reading the code before any test saw it (no test races a rename against a delete).
- **M6.2:** no bug in the shipped behaviour was found by the tests, the 15 mutation checks, the stress and chaos runs or the browser run. Findings and slips on the way: (1) **a real bug caught by the first smoke test:** `GET /admin/stats` answered 500 because the assignment-time query selected only aggregates, so SQLAlchemy did not know which table the join started from; fixed with `select_from(Ride)`, and the tests cover it. (2) **autogenerate missed the seven new check constraints** (it never sees checks on an existing table), as in M5.4 and M6.1; they are written by hand in `0011`. (3) The migration file created inside the container belonged to root, so it could not be edited from the host until its owner was changed. (4) **14 existing tests used values the new checks forbid** (a minimum fare below 100, a surge cap of 0.5); they were fixed as listed under "Admin dashboard results" instead of loosening the checks. (5) The first wording of the Pricing page said that a ride in progress is settled with the new per-km and per-minute rates; reading `calculate_fare` showed that the whole rule is re-read, so base fare and minimum fare also apply, and the text, the README and this file say so. (6) In the browser the drivers table first said "offline, offline"; the wording was fixed. (7) Slips in throwaway scripts, none in the repo: a `pkill -f` that matched its own shell (twice), a SQL column that was ambiguous, a first live timing taken while the simulator was still logging in 200 drivers (re-measured), a hash test that changed only the URL fragment (the page rightly ignores that until a reload), and clicks that hit a leftover ride on the same coordinates.
- **M6.1:** no bug in the shipped code was found by the tests, the 11 mutation checks, the stress and chaos runs or the browser run. Slips on the way: (1) **the migration ran without its hand-written parts:** `alembic revision --autogenerate` ran inside the container, so the file was owned by root and my edit (the two CHECKs and the backfill, which Alembic cannot see) failed with a permission error, yet `upgrade head` ran the generated file; found by `\d ratings` showing no new check. Fixed by downgrading, copying the file back as the normal user, editing it and upgrading again (the same slip as M5.1: edit the file BEFORE the first `upgrade`). (2) **a wrong expectation in my own test:** 999 over 200 is 4.995, which rounds half up to 5.0, not 4.99; the code was right. (3) **a throwaway mutation script ran nothing** the first time (it compared letters with full names), so the run printed nothing; noticed because the output was empty. (4) **`pkill -f` killed its own shell:** a command that started with `pkill -f "simulator/fake_stripe.py"` and then started the simulator also matched itself, so the simulator never started and the 10 regression rides ended NO_DRIVER_FOUND; repeated with the simulator started on its own. (5) **a vacuous browser check:** Playwright's sync API only delivers network events while one of its calls runs, so `time.sleep(10)` followed by "no rating requests happened" counted zero polls and passed for nothing; found because the same check printed `polls 0`; fixed with `page.wait_for_timeout`. The NO_DRIVER_FOUND browser step also failed at first because the page only learns of a ride from its own POST or from `/rides/active` (404 for a finished one), so a ride created through the API is invisible to it; the step now requests that ride through the page's map. (6) The `riders` stress run printed two 422s: one rider's random pickup and drop-off were too close together and the same request was sent twice; not a defect.
- **M5.4:** no bug in the shipped code was found by the tests, the 10 mutation checks, the stress and chaos runs or the browser run. Findings and slips on the way: (1) **a slip that briefly broke the dev database's migration:** `alembic revision --autogenerate` runs inside the backend container, so the new file is owned by root and my edit of it (the hand-written check constraint, the backfill and the guard) failed with a permission error, while the next command had already applied the raw autogenerated version (table and column, no backfill). Found at once from the error; fixed by `alembic downgrade -1`, replacing the file (the directory is mine, so the root-owned file could be removed and recreated), and upgrading again; the numbers were then checked as above, and the round trip proved the final file. (2) **Autogenerate again missed the CHECK constraint on the existing `pricing_rules` table** (the same limit as M5.2 and M5.3): written by hand in the migration and proved by the forbidden-insert check. (3) **The foreign key adds a lock the design said was absent:** the earning insert takes no explicit lock, but Postgres takes a KEY SHARE lock on the driver's row for the `driver_id` foreign key until the commit. It conflicts only with `FOR UPDATE` on that row (accept, matching's `SKIP LOCKED`), the settlement waits for nothing that accept holds, and the chaos runs (four of them, one with two processes) saw no deadlock, no 503 and no stuck ride; recorded in `CLAUDE.md` and the open issues. (4) Observed, not a bug: a rolled-back settlement still uses a payment id (the gap at 906), because sequences are not transactional. (5) Mistakes in my throwaway checks, not the app: the first by-hand ride waited 4 minutes for a 30 km/h trip that takes about 8 (approach plus trip); a single simulated driver answers offers at random (70 percent accept) and one ride ended NO_DRIVER_FOUND, so the single-driver runs used `--accept-rate 1 --reject-rate 0`; `kill -INT` on the first process found by `ps` once targeted the wrong process and left a simulator running; in the browser script a map click made while the page was busy is ignored by design (`onMapClick` returns while `state.busy`), the page had scrolled so a click point was outside the viewport, each finished ride refits the map so the next pickup drifted out of the driver's 3 km, Playwright's `time.sleep` delays request events (use `wait_for_timeout` when counting requests), and an early expectation of 20 entries counted rows that were not there. (6) No existing test needed a change, so the new tests passed on their first run (53 of 53); the 10 mutation checks were therefore not optional, and all 10 were caught.
- **M5.3:** no bug in the shipped code was found by the tests, the 12 mutation checks, the stress runs or the browser run. Findings and slips on the way: (1) **a design bug found while writing the fake:** the first version sent `expires_at = now + 1 hour` in the Checkout form, and a real Stripe refuses a retry with the same `Idempotency-Key` whose parameters differ, so a retry after a crash would have been 502 for ever; it is now derived from the row's `created_at`. (2) **`text()` bind parameters:** the migration's data statement contains `':charge'`, which `text()` reads as a parameter; it is escaped as `\:charge` (the downgrade too). (3) **File ownership:** `alembic revision` run as root inside the container made a root-owned file that could not be edited on the host; it was copied and replaced (next time use `docker compose exec --user $(id -u):$(id -g)`). (4) **The `payments` stress scenario's first check was wrong, not the app:** of the three deliveries of the second event id the FIRST is `ignored` (a new event id, the top-up already credited) and the other two are `duplicate`, so a top-up sees 1 `processed`, 1 `ignored` and 11 `duplicate`; the checker and the README were fixed. (5) **The fake was unreachable from the container** while bound to 127.0.0.1: it needs `--host 0.0.0.0` on Linux. (6) Test-code slips: a rollback expires the ORM objects of a session (reading their `id` afterwards needs I/O), and a helper that patched `httpx.AsyncClient` captured an already patched client on its second use. (7) `pkill -f fake_stripe` also matched the shell command that held the pattern and killed it. (8) In the browser script the page ignores a map click while the first one is still handled (`state.busy`), so the script waits between the two clicks; a stray offer went to a simulator driver while the fleet was running, so the click-through ran with the fleet stopped.
- **M5.2:** no bug in the shipped code was found by the tests, the 12 mutation checks, the stress runs, or the chaos runs. Findings and slips on the way: (1) **Alembic autogenerate does not see CHECK constraints:** the generated migration had the two columns and the index but not `ck_rides_surge_percent_range`; it was added by hand and checked in psql (`UPDATE ... surge_percent = 99` fails with the constraint name, downgrade removes it, a second autogenerate is empty). The file was also created by root inside the container, so it was replaced with a copy owned by me. (2) **A mutation survived at first:** surging the cancellation fee after the driver arrived was not caught, because no test cancelled a surged ride at that stage; `test_cancelling_after_the_driver_arrived_is_not_surged_either` was added (and the late-cancel test now covers both "the zone surges" and "the ride was surged"). (3) **Offer-wave spike (a behavior, not a bug):** while a burst of requests waits for drivers' answers, the REQUESTED riders count as demand and the drivers with pending offers leave the supply, so the zone can show 2.0x for 10 to 20 seconds even with 40 drivers (see Surge results and the open issues). (4) Throwaway-script slips, none in the app: a script started twice by a paste mistake (more demand rides, harmless), `pkill -f` killing my own shell because the command line contained the pattern (twice), the Docker log marker given in UTC instead of local time, a lab login call given a tuple, and a first attempt of the cancellation-fee run where the last simulator drivers (presence lasts 30 s after a stop) were still online and took the ride, so it was repeated after the presence keys were gone.
- **M5.1:** no bug in the shipped code was found by the tests, stress, or chaos runs. Slips on the way: (1) the first `alembic upgrade` ran the autogenerated migration WITHOUT the legacy data statement, because the file was created by root inside the container and my rewrite failed with `Permission denied`; fixed by `alembic downgrade -1`, deleting the file (the directory is mine), rewriting it, and upgrading again. (2) The first run of the 8 mutation checks reported "no test failed" for the driver-fee mutation: the patch string lacked the leading indentation, so the file had a syntax error and no test ran; the corrected mutation fails 2 tests. (3) The spec says a ping 1 fake second after the previous one is ignored, but the rule (`now - ts < MIN_PING_GAP_S`) ignores only pings LESS than 1 s later, so `test_a_ping_too_soon_is_ignored...` uses 0.5 s (ignored) and exactly 1 s (counted).
- **M4.3:** no bug in the backend was found by the new tests or by any stress or chaos run (the constraints never fired, the sweeper never failed outside an outage). Findings and slips in the new code and tools: (1) **a rewritten test, not a bug:** `test_two_offers_of_one_driver_accepted_at_once_assign_one_ride` inserted two PENDING offers for one driver and now violates `uq_ride_offers_one_pending_per_driver`; rewritten (see Tests). (2) **My own wrong expectation:** in the "one failing offer" test I expected the third ride to end NO_DRIVER_FOUND; it was offered onward instead, because the second driver's offer had just been closed in the same pass and that driver was free again. The sweeper was right. (3) **Duplicated log lines:** attaching caplog to uvicorn's logger delivered each record twice (the logger also propagates to the root logger); the `logged` fixture turns propagation off for the test. (4) **`MissingGreenlet` in a test:** after `db.rollback()` the test's user objects are expired and reading `.id` tries IO; ids are read up front, as `test_offer_constraints` already did. (5) **Tooling:** `docker compose logs --since 2026-10-09T08:34:11` is read as LOCAL time, so a UTC string pulled in 5 hours of old lines (it looked like sweeper failures from an earlier probe); the run script now passes a Unix timestamp. A detached `docker compose exec -d` process writes nowhere `docker compose logs` can see, so the second uvicorn now runs as `sh -c 'uvicorn ... > /tmp/p2.log 2>&1'` and its warnings are counted from that file. (6) **A restart kills the second uvicorn** with the container, so the restart run used one process. (7) The first draft of the sweeper tests counted calls to `get_online_ids` that matching itself makes while the test set up its rides; the spy is installed after the setup.
- **M4.1:** no bug in the backend (none was changed); the bug being measured is the known race. Two bugs in the NEW tool were found by the checks and fixed. (1) **Ctrl+C left rides behind:** the first interrupt test (SIGINT in the middle of a 100-rider round) printed the summary but psql still showed 6 active stress rides. Cause: the in-flight `POST /rides` requests are not cancelled by the server when the client goes away, and they create rides after the cleanup's single listing. Fix: the cleanup repeats until a query finds no active ride, and after an interrupt or a failure it waits 3 s first (three interrupts afterwards: 0 left). (2) **`--cleanup-only` took 0 drivers offline** after that fix: the logins of the stress drivers sat inside the loop that ends early when no ride is found. Found by the rerun of the control and cleanup checks, fixed by logging in before the early exit, and rechecked with Redis (presence keys and GEO members of the stress drivers gone). Also: a precedence slip (`a | b - c`) in the set of accounts to log in was caught while rereading, before the first run. Dev state found on the first run: 5 REQUESTED rides from 2026-10-08 (before offers existed in M3.3, so with no offer) made I4 fail on a clean database; they were cancelled through the API with short-lived tokens made inside the backend container (no file, no SQL write). Mistakes in my throwaway checks, not the tool: `timeout -s INT` signalled the harness shell and lost the output; `setsid ... &` made `$!` the wrapper's pid, so the first SIGINT went nowhere and the tool kept running to the next round; the first evidence query for driver 256 also returned the earlier rounds because the stress drivers are reused (the second query filtered by id); a `python3 -I` "httpx missing" check was invalid because the system Python has httpx (the import was blocked with `sys.modules` instead). Not exercised: Ctrl+C on Windows, and the `fleet` exit-2 path for "no sim-driver users" (this database has them).
- **M3.5:** no bug in the app code was found by the checks. (1) The first full run of the old tests failed 3, as the design predicts, and they were updated rather than the code: a rider cancel after the accept now also sends the driver `ride_updated` (the old test expected total silence on the driver's socket), and two "nothing is published after the ride ended" tracking tests now see the `ride_updated` events of the status changes made before the ping (they drain the spy first). (2) The new test file passed on its first run (51 of 51), so the four mutation checks were not optional; all four were caught. (3) Mistakes in my throwaway checks, not the app: a helper script named `token.py` shadowed the standard library module and crashed Python at import, and then needed `PYTHONPATH=/app` because it lived in `/tmp`; the first "A is matchable again" check ended NO_DRIVER_FOUND because my test driver does not ping and its 30 s presence had expired (a ping never brings an offline driver back, so the heartbeat loop needed a go-online first); the `--reload` restarts caused by my mutation edits closed my listener sockets with 1012; restarting Redis for the outage check emptied dev Redis (no persistence), so the dev drivers went offline; a `driver_location` line from my heartbeat looked like a message during the "nothing is published" cancel checks (the ride was IN_PROGRESS and the driver was pinging; there was no `ride_updated`); and `pkill -f` matched my own shell again (as in M2.4, M3.1, M3.2, and M3.4), which made the harness report exit code 1 for a simulator that had stopped normally. (4) Browser script findings that were not bugs: the Driver tooltip was still found at the instant the rider page said "Trip completed" because Leaflet fades a tooltip out for 200 ms (gone 209 ms later); and one click-through measured 3.2 s between the driver's Cancel click and the rider's note, which was Playwright handling the `confirm()` dialog (the rider's socket got `ride_updated` 50 to 70 ms after the cancel response in two instrumented runs). (5) Observed, not a bug: the 404 lines in the browser console while the pages poll are `/rides/active` and `/drivers/me/offer` answering "nothing yet".
- **M3.4:** no bug in the app code was found by the checks. (1) The first logic harness run passed 35 of 35, so it was mutation-checked against 12 broken copies of `ws.js`; one survived (removing the jitter, because a delay of exactly the cap is still inside `[cap/2, cap]`), so the backoff test now pins the two ends (`Math.random` forced to 0 must wait exactly `cap / 2`, and just under 1 must wait about `cap`) and then caught it. (2) The host has no Node, so the harnesses ran in a throwaway `node:22-slim` container with `--network host`; its control file was first created as root and the host could not append to it (fixed with `--user`). (3) A shell chain with `grep -c ... && pkill ...` stopped early because `grep -c` exits 1 on zero matches, and `pkill -f` matched my own shell again, as in M2.4 and M3.1. (4) Observed, not a bug: while the backend restarts or is stopped, connects HANG (they are not refused), so the 10 s connect watchdog does most of the work in an outage.

- **M3.3:** no bug in the app code was found by the checks. (1) Writing the model: the naming convention builds a unique constraint name from the FIRST column only (`uq_ride_offers_ride_id`), so the (ride_id, driver_id) constraint is named explicitly, as `ratings` already does. (2) An early draft of the throwaway shell checks expanded `$$` (a Postgres dollar-quote) into the shell's PID and silently looped on SQL errors; moved the SQL to files read from stdin. (3) The browser click-through first failed three times on its own mistakes, not the app: after a ride the rider map stays fitted to the old route, so a click at "the map centre" was no longer the pickup (the script now reloads the rider page before each request); a driver moved by hand during a trip stayed 3.6 km from the next pickup (out of the 3 km radius); and a crashed run left drivers whose presence lived for another 30 s, so the next run's "nearest" driver was a ghost of the previous one. A health-poll loop also crashed on a connection reset while the backend restarted. (4) The first run of the contention check proved nothing: the extra sweeper script was started with the wrong `PYTHONPATH` and died at import; rerun with three working sweepers.
- **M3.2:** no bug in the app code, but two limits found. (1) **A hidden tab jumps once when it returns:** `applyDriverLocation` starts each glide from where the marker is drawn, and while frames are paused the drawn position never changes, so every update restarts from the same stale spot. Emulated by holding `requestAnimationFrame` for 20 s (7 updates piled up): after release the marker made ONE 206 px jump (about 60 m at maximum zoom), then was at the current position about 2.2 s later and glided normally. It is bounded (the move must be under 500 m, otherwise it snaps anyway) and follows the fixed design; a fix would start the glide from the position the previous animation would have reached by now. Not changed. (2) The details fetch of a ride is not retried (open issues). Mistakes in my throwaway checks, not the app: the first headless run reported 5 failures that were measurement errors (tooltip positions are whole pixels at low zoom so "frames moving" meant nothing; one drift sample included frames after the driver left Leaflet's renderer bounds, where Leaflet empties the path; a reduced-motion check began while a glide was still running; the marker needs a few seconds to settle after ARRIVED because the page lags up to 3 s; and straight-line distance to the drop-off grows at the start of a trip because the road first bends away), each re-measured with a sound metric; a click-on-the-marker check was vacuous because the driver already stood at the pickup, redone on the drop-off marker; a CDP `Page.setWebLifecycleState` freeze did not seem to freeze the page, so the hidden-tab check holds `requestAnimationFrame` instead (headless Chrome never hides a tab); `pkill -f` and `ps | grep` patterns matched my own shell again (twice), and crashed scripts left simulators and rides behind that had to be stopped by hand.
- **M3.1:** (1) **Design flaw found by the stalled-client check:** the listener sends one message at a time under a 5 s timeout, so one client that stops reading froze delivery to every other user for 9.99 s (5 s for the stuck send, then 5 s for the stuck `close()` of the same socket). The spec asked that a stalled client "must never block" others; the two-function structure it also asked for cannot do both. Not fixed: accepted as a known limit on 2026-10-09 (open issues). (2) The first version of the stalled-client test passed vacuously: a `websockets` library client keeps reading in the background even with `max_queue=1` or `transport.pause_reading()`, so even 100 MB (1000 x 100 KB) never made the server block. Fixed by a hand-made TCP client (raw handshake, masked auth frame, 4 KB receive buffer, reads nothing). (3) That test hung pytest for minutes when an assertion failed: the stalled client's socket was still open and uvicorn's shutdown waited for it. Fixed with `writer.close()` in a `finally`; whether a real shutdown with a stuck client also waits was not tested (compose would SIGKILL after 10 s). (4) Mistakes in my throwaway checks, not the app: a bare `redis-cli` with no arguments waited on stdin and hung the shell, `pkill -f` and `/proc` greps that matched themselves, a script run from `/tmp/t` could not import `app` until `PYTHONPATH=/app` was set, and a 125-byte limit in my hand-written frame header (the auth message needs the 2-byte length form).
- **M2.5:** (1) A driver stood still for minutes: its log said `route: no route` once and nothing else. Cause: its random start snapped (within 300 m) onto a road that is not connected to the rest of the network, so every wander route failed. Found by the Redis/OSRM outage check (one of 30 drivers never recovered); fix: a start point must also have a route to the fleet center, and the center must be near a road. (2) The first run printed every HTTP request because httpx logs at INFO; silenced. (3) 3 failed pings and 5 failed polls with `RemoteProtocolError` in the first 200 driver run: httpx's idle connection expiry (5 s) equals uvicorn's keep-alive timeout; fixed with `keepalive_expiry=2` (none in later runs; the fresh 150 account registration at 200 drivers was not repeated after the fix). (4) Mistakes in the verification scripts, not the simulator: a `pkill -f` pattern that matched its own shell (again, as in M2.4), a "far from a road" test point that was 90 m from a road and so started a real fleet in the foreground, an env file without `export`, and a Python script whose output was buffered until it exited.
- **M2.4:** no bug in the matching code. (1) Caught by reading the rider page before writing: it learned the ride id only from `/rides/active`, which answers 404 for a NO_DRIVER_FOUND ride, so the finished view would never have appeared; fixed by taking the ride from the `POST /rides` answer. (2) Converting the tests: `test_pricing.py` also needed two small edits (a ride with nobody online is now NO_DRIVER_FOUND, and the 409-before-routing test needs a ride inserted directly). (3) Click-through script mistake, not an app bug: after a ride the rider map is fitted to the route, so "click the map centre" no longer meant the pickup and a driver 2 km away was out of range; the script now reloads the rider page before a repeat request. (4) `pkill -f` in a command that contained the pattern killed its own shell.
- **M2.3:** no bug in the app code; the browser click-through and curl checks passed. Two mistakes in the throwaway click-through script, not in the app: it used a page after closing it, and it compared `redis-cli` output to `(integer) 0`, which only appears with a terminal (piped output is plain `0`).
- **M2.2:** (1) After picking two places the map ended up zoomed in at street level with the route running off the screen, instead of fitted. Cause: clicking a search result calls `setView(place, 16)`, which starts an animated zoom; the estimate answer arrives about 50 ms later and `fitBounds` runs during that animation, and Leaflet's `_tryAnimatedZoom` silently returns without doing anything while a zoom animation is running. Found by the browser click-through (route bounding box larger than the map, confirmed with a screenshot). Fix: all view changes made from code use `animate: false`. (2) `docker pull ...:v26.10.0` failed with "not found" although GitHub has that release: since 26.4.1 the registry only publishes distro-suffixed tags, so the image is `v26.10.0-debian`. (3) `OSM_EXTRACT_URL` in `.env` did not break the backend, so no `config.py` change was needed (settings ignore unknown environment variables). (4) Two click-through script mistakes, not app bugs: the mock for `/places/**` also caught `/places/map-config`, and Playwright waits forever on Leaflet's disabled zoom-out button at minimum zoom (also seen in M2.1).
- **M2.1:** no bug in the app code. Two things worth knowing: (1) the leafletjs.com quick-start page no longer shows Leaflet 1.9.4 (it documents 2.0 alpha), so the integrity hashes could not be copied from it; they were checked against Leaflet's docs at the 1.9.4 release commit instead (see the decisions log). (2) The click-through script first failed on its own checks: Leaflet snaps the map center to whole pixels (about 1e-5 degrees off) and a third zoom-out click hits the disabled button at min zoom 10. Both were test mistakes, not app bugs.
- **M1.4:** none in the frontend. The browser click-through passed on its first full run. Expect red 404 lines in the browser console while polling: they are `/rides/active` and `/drivers/me` answering "nothing yet", which the pages treat as normal.
- **M1.3:** the first version of the 49-pair transition test used the app's own `ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` as the expected answer, so a deliberate wrong change to the dict (REQUESTED to COMPLETED) still passed all 69 tests. The mutation check caught it. Fix: the test now has its own hand-written list of legal pairs. Also: `pytest` could not import `app` until `pythonpath = .` was added to `pytest.ini`.
- **M1.2:** none. Note for later: a failed insert (lost race) still consumes an id from the sequence, so ids are not gapless (a race test skipped driver id 4).
- **M0.2:** `alembic revision --autogenerate` produced a migration that dropped ~40 PostGIS tables (`tiger.*`, `topology.*`, `spatial_ref_sys`). Cause: the `postgis/postgis` image creates them, so Alembic sees them as tables missing from our models. Fix: `include_object` filter in `alembic/env.py`.
